  - [ADVISORY] Figure is not self-contained: $N$, $W$, and the per-curve $\nu$ values live only in the caption and in a cross-referenced table.
  - [ADVISORY] Two-blue palette (multitaper vs. Bohman) is not robust to greyscale reproduction or to common forms of colour vision deficiency; these are the two curves whose separation carries the main message of panel (b).
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (a) y-axis top tick is 0.10 while data reach ~0.13, so the multitaper ripple amplitude cannot be quantified; the near-coincident box/Hann/Bohman plateaus are also unresolvable at this scale.
  - [ADVISORY] Panels (a) and (b) share an axis label but not an axis range or a normalization; this is not flagged in the caption.
SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Extend the panel (b) ordinate to $-120$ dB (or to the computed minimum) so no curve is clipped; if a floor is retained, annotate it explicitly, e.g. "curves clipped at $-100$ dB," and mark clipped segments.
  - Replace "the skirts differ by 40 dB" with an offset-specific statement, e.g. "at $|{\rm offset}| = 10/N$ the Bohman skirt is ~$X$ dB below the untapered skirt," and give the range over which the separation grows.
  - Recompute all kernels on a finer frequency grid (or zero-pad to a much longer DFT) to remove the staircase artifacts; state the evaluation grid spacing in the caption.
  - Annotate $N = 256$ and $W = 4/N$ directly in one panel, and append each curve's $\nu$ to its legend entry (e.g. "multitaper, $K=7$, $\nu=14.0$"; "Hann, $\nu=8.9$") so the degrees-of-freedom comparison is readable without the table.
  - Recolour so that multitaper and Bohman are clearly separable (e.g. distinct hue plus distinct dash pattern), and verify legibility in greyscale and under a deuteranopia simulation.
  - Add an inset to panel (a), or extend its ordinate with a tick at 0.15, to resolve the plateau region where the box, Hann, and Bohman curves overlap; draw the dotted ideal box on top of the solid curves, or use a slightly heavier dotted stroke, so it is not occluded.
  - State the two x-ranges and the two normalizations explicitly in the caption, e.g. "(a) linear, normalized by $N$, $|{\rm offset}| \le 10/N$; (b) dB re each kernel's own peak, $|{\rm offset}| \le 24/N$."
  - Consider marking $\pm W$ with light vertical guides in both panels (currently only implied by the dotted box) so main-lobe width equality is verifiable by eye.
---

### efigure2.png

---

**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Line traces over a continuous frequency axis are the correct display for PSD estimates; no bar/pie misuse. One caveat: because ν (equivalent degrees of freedom) is quoted in the caption for every estimator, the figure is carrying a variance claim that it does not display. Consider adding shaded χ²-based confidence bands (or at least a single representative CI bar, since the dB-scale CI width is frequency-independent) for one or two estimators so the bias–variance trade-off being argued is visible, not just asserted.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — Neither *N* = 1024 nor *W* = 4/N appears in the figure; both are caption-only. More importantly, this is a **single realization** (effectively n = 1). All statements about "floors 20 dB high" / "halves that" are properties of the expected value of the estimator, but the reader sees one draw, where realization noise of ±5–10 dB is plainly visible in panel (b). Either annotate *N*, *W*, *K*, and "single realization" on the panel, or add a small inset/companion showing the Monte-Carlo mean bias over many realizations to support the bias claims.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — Two distinct problems:
1. **K = 7 vs K = 6 are not visually separable.** The dashed blue (K = 6) is drawn under/over the solid blue (K = 7) in the same hue, and in the dense high-frequency region both collapse into a single filled blue band. The caption's central claim — "dropping the seventh taper halves that" (10 dB → ~5 dB) — cannot be read off either panel. In panel (b) the blue band spans roughly 5–13 dB with no way to attribute the upper edge to K = 7 and the lower edge to K = 6.
2. These estimators are all computed from the **same realization** (paired by construction). The comparison is therefore paired, and the most informative view is a difference between estimators, not two overlaid noisy traces. Panel (b) already differences against the truth; a K7 − K6 difference trace (or a separate panel restricted to f > 0.2) would make the paired structure legible and remove the common realization noise that currently dominates.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — Two items to check:
- Panel (a): the grey periodogram appears to be **clipped at the lower axis limit** (~−30 dB); periodogram troughs for a single realization routinely reach −40 dB or lower. Clipped data silently understates periodogram variance, which is the very quantity the figure is contrasting. Either extend the limit or state that the axis is cropped.
- Panels (a) and (b) use different y-ranges and different tick spacings (20 dB vs 5 dB), so the same dB difference occupies ~4× more vertical space in (b). This is legitimate (they show different quantities) but amplifies the apparent bias in (b); a note or a matched-scale inset would be fairer.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Severe in the f > 0.25 region of both panels. The rapidly oscillating blue traces render as a solid filled block, obliterating the dashed/solid distinction and the green/orange traces where they cross it. Jitter/transparency are not applicable to line data; the appropriate fixes are (i) reduce the plotted frequency resolution or apply light smoothing for display, (ii) split the crowded estimators into small multiples, (iii) plot envelopes (min/max or quantile ribbons) instead of raw oscillating lines, or (iv) add an inset zoom on a narrow band (e.g. 0.30–0.35) at full resolution where the K = 7 vs K = 6 gap can actually be seen.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential annotations or p-values; the figure is illustrative/methodological. No correction issue arises.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — Frequency-domain display on a complete, evenly spaced grid from 0 to 0.5 cycles/sample; no gaps or interpolation across missing points.

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] The K = 6 (dashed blue) trace is not distinguishable from K = 7 (solid blue) anywhere in the figure. The caption's principal quantitative claim ("dropping the seventh taper halves that") is unverifiable from the graphic as drawn.
- [BLOCKING] Caption–figure numerical mismatch: the caption states the untapered-box estimate "floors 20 dB high" and K = 7 "floors 10 dB high," but panel (b) shows the green curve plateauing near 18 dB and the blue band centred nearer 10–13 dB. Reconcile the stated values with the plotted values, or qualify them as approximate.
- [ADVISORY] Apparent clipping of the grey periodogram at the bottom of panel (a) — verify and disclose or extend the axis.
- [ADVISORY] Sample size / process parameters (N, W, K, single realization) are caption-only; the panel is not self-contained.
- [ADVISORY] ν values are quoted for all five estimators but no variance information is rendered, so the reader cannot assess the bias–variance trade-off being argued.
- [ADVISORY] The two blue traces differ only by line style within the same hue; under print reduction or for readers with reduced acuity this distinction is effectively lost. Green/orange/blue are otherwise a reasonable colourblind-safe set.
- [ADVISORY] Different y-scales across panels (20 dB vs 5 dB ticks) visually magnify bias in panel (b).

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Give K = 6 a clearly distinct encoding: a different hue (e.g. light blue or purple) rather than a dashed variant of the same blue, and/or plot K = 7 and K = 6 in separate stacked panels.
- Add a third panel or inset showing K7 − K6 directly (paired difference on a common realization), which cancels realization noise and makes the "halving" claim readable.
- Add an inset zoom on a narrow high-frequency band (e.g. 0.30–0.35) at full resolution to resolve the currently over-plotted region; alternatively plot display-smoothed traces or quantile envelopes in the f > 0.25 region.
- Overlay χ²-based confidence bands (or one representative CI bar with its ν) for at least the multitaper and Hann-box estimates.
- Annotate N = 1024, W = 4/N, K, and "single realization" directly on panel (a); if the bias claims are meant as expectations, add a Monte-Carlo mean-bias curve (with the number of realizations stated) so the claim is supported by more than n = 1.
- Extend the panel (a) lower y-limit until the grey periodogram is no longer clipped, or state explicitly that troughs are cropped for legibility.
- Recheck and align the caption's "20 dB" and "10 dB" figures with the plotted plateaus; state whether they refer to this realization or to the expected bias.
- Consider harmonising tick spacing between panels, or add a note that panel (b) uses an expanded dB scale.

### efigure3.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Multi-panel line/marker plots of exact, deterministic kernel and degrees-of-freedom computations swept over a parameter. There is no sampling distribution to display, so bar/box/violin alternatives do not apply. Markers at each evaluated parameter value are shown, which is the correct practice for a swept-parameter curve: the reader can see exactly where computations were performed and where the lines are interpolation. Log scale in (b) and dB in (c) are appropriate for quantities spanning many orders of magnitude.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — $N=256$ appears only in the caption, not in the figure. This is not "sample size" in the inferential sense (the curves are analytic, with no replicates and no error bars, correctly so), but $N$ is the single most important determinant of every curve shown and should be legible from the graphic alone. The number and spacing of swept bandwidth values is inferable from the plotted markers, which is good.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — The design decision to plot against *measured* half-power width rather than nominal bandwidth parameter is the right way to compare methods at equal resolution, and is clearly explained. One substantive concern: panel (c) plots a quantity that is not the same physical thing for all curves (side-lobe peak for flat-top kernels vs. main-lobe skirt for Gaussian and Welch). The caption discloses this, but a reader scanning the figure will read the pink and orange curves as "very high side lobes" when they are a different measurement. This should be marked in the figure itself.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None — Panel (a) has a linear $y$-axis anchored at 0, which is correct. Panels (b) and (c) use log/dB scales where a zero origin is meaningless; their lower limits are set by the data, not chosen to exaggerate. The $x$-axes start near the smallest achievable width rather than 0, which is legitimate for a resolution coordinate. No misleading truncation.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (minor) — In panel (a) the three multitaper curves ($K=2NW-1$, $K=2NW-2$, sine tapers) and the untapered-then-box curve run nearly coincident from $x\approx 2$ to $x\approx 10$; distinguishing them relies on marker shape and dash pattern at small size. In panel (c) six curves cross repeatedly in the $-15$ to $-30$ dB band. Not fatal, since line style and marker already carry the encoding redundantly with colour, but density is high.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No hypothesis tests, $p$-values, or significance annotations are present, and none are appropriate for deterministic computations.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa is half-power width, not time. However, the analogous *interpolation-across-gaps* concern does apply; see ISSUES.

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] The $y$-axis labels of panels (b) and (c) are clipped/overlapped by the adjacent panel: "kernel power beyond $2\times$ half-power half-width" reads as "ernel power…" and "peak beyond $1.5\times$ half-power half-width (dB)" reads as "eak beyond…". The axis quantities are therefore not recoverable from the figure alone. Increase inter-panel spacing or shorten the labels.
- [ADVISORY] Misleading interpolation across a wide gap: in panel (a) the Welch/Hann 50%-overlap curve is drawn as a single straight segment from roughly $(8, 17)$ to $(24.6, 60)$, implying a linear relationship over a region where no computation was performed. The same occurs, less severely, for the pink curves in (b) and (c) between $x\approx 10$ and $x\approx 25$.
- [ADVISORY] Curves terminate at different $x$ values (Hann-then-Gaussian stops near $x\approx 10$; multitaper and box-smoothed curves near $x\approx 16$; Welch extends to $x\approx 25$) with no explanation. A reader cannot tell whether the method is infeasible beyond that width, whether the sweep was simply not extended, or whether values were suppressed.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (c) mixes two distinct measurands (side-lobe peak vs. main-lobe skirt) on one axis with no in-figure indication of which curves are which.
- [ADVISORY] $N=256$ is not stated in the figure; the dotted vertical line at $8/N$ is unlabelled within the plot area.
- [ADVISORY] Near-coincident multitaper curves in panel (a) are hard to separate at print size.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Increase horizontal spacing between the three panels (or move the $y$-labels to a rotated outer position / use shorter labels such as "leakage beyond $2\,W_{1/2}$" and "peak beyond $1.5\,W_{1/2}$ (dB)") so no axis text is clipped.
- Do not draw straight lines across large unsampled gaps. Either add intermediate computed points for Welch between $x\approx 8$ and $x\approx 25$, or render the long segment as a lighter/dashed "interpolated" link so readers see that no data lie between the endpoints.
- Add a sentence to the caption, and ideally a short in-figure note, stating why each curve terminates where it does (e.g. maximum achievable width for that method at $N=256$).
- Distinguish the "main-lobe skirt" curves in panel (c) from the true side-lobe curves — e.g. open markers, a hatched band, or a second legend group labelled "no flat top: main-lobe skirt" — so the caption caveat survives without the caption.
- Annotate $N=256$ inside panel (a) and label the dotted vertical line directly (e.g. "$8/N$, Table 1") in at least one panel.
- To separate the coincident multitaper curves in (a), consider an inset zoom over $2 \le x \le 10$, or plot $\nu$ normalised by its ideal value $2\times$(width$\times N$) so the near-degenerate curves spread apart.
- State explicitly in the caption that no error bars appear because the curves are exact computations rather than estimates from simulated realisations; this pre-empts the reader's assumption of omitted uncertainty.

### efigure4.png

---
**Context note:** This is a methods/theory figure displaying deterministic quantities (taper sequences, eigenvalue spectra, analytic kernel responses) computed from closed-form window definitions. There are no sampled observations, no replicates, and no inferential comparisons. Most items in the statistical-integrity checklist are therefore not applicable, and the "bar chart without individual points" rule specifically **does not** apply to panels (i)–(l), which plot exact eigen-weights $c_k$ rather than group means. The review below concentrates on the items that do carry over: comparability of shared axes, truncation/clipping, and legibility.

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots for time-domain tapers (a–h) and log-magnitude kernels (m–p) are the conventional and correct choices. Bar charts in (i)–(l) represent a discrete, exhaustive, deterministic index set (one bar per taper $k$); no distribution is being concealed because there is no sampling distribution. No error bars are shown, which is correct here and should not be "fixed" by adding them.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not applicable — No $n$ exists. The analogous quantities that *do* need to be stated are the record length $N$, the design bandwidth $W$, and the number of retained tapers $K$. $K$ is given only for column (d) ($K=7$); for (a)–(c) the effective $K$ must be read off the bar counts in (i)–(k) (7, ~12, ~10). $N$ appears nowhere in the figure despite the x-axes of (a)–(h) running 0–~256 in samples and all frequency axes being in units of $1/N$.

COMPARISON:         Valid — Cross-column comparison is the explicit purpose, and it is supported properly: panels (i)–(l) share identical y-limits (0–0.45) and x-limits (≈−1 to 12), and (m)–(p) share identical y-limits (−80 to ~+5) and x-limits (±25/N). This is good practice and should be preserved. One residual concern: the degrees-of-freedom values $\nu$ (13.4, 11.1, 17.2, 14.0) are compared across columns whose kernel bandwidths differ (5.8/N to 8.0/N), so $\nu$ and resolution trade off; the figure invites a "more $\nu$ is better" reading of (k) that the wider kernel in (o) partly negates. The caption should state this coupling explicitly.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    Present — Two instances, both benign in intent but worth one explicit statement. (1) In (m) and (n) the traces run off the bottom of the −80 dB floor (the Hann-segment sidelobe nulls and the Gaussian skirt), so the visible dynamic range is clipped rather than exhausted; the reader cannot tell whether the floor is at −80 or far lower. (2) In (a)–(h) the y-axis is unlabelled and untickked with arbitrary per-curve vertical offsets ("curves offset"), so relative amplitudes between traces within a panel and between panels are not readable. Neither truncation exaggerates a group difference, but (1) should be acknowledged and (2) should be declared as offset-with-arbitrary-scale so no one infers amplitude information from it. Notably, (i)–(l) correctly begin at zero.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — Mild, and confined to labelling rather than data density. In (d) the "k = 1" legend entry sits on top of the $k=0$ trace; in (b), (c) and (d) the panel letter collides with the column title text above it, and in (c) the "(c)" glyph overlaps the title. In (h) and (p) the four/one traces are clean. In (p) the rapid sidelobe oscillation (~40 lobes across the axis) is near the resolution limit of the rendered line width and will alias in print at reduced size.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No hypothesis tests, p-values, or significance annotations appear anywhere in the figure. No correction issue arises.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — The x-axis of (a)–(h) is sample index of a deterministic window function on a complete, uniform grid; there are no missing timepoints and no interpolation across gaps.

ISSUES:
  - [ADVISORY] $N$ is never given numerically, yet every frequency axis is expressed in units of $1/N$ and every kernel width is quoted as a multiple of $1/N$. The figure is not self-contained without it.
  - [ADVISORY] $W$ is not given numerically, although the caption says the shading marks $|f|\le W$ and all four titles quote "% beyond $2W$." The shaded band appears to span roughly $\pm 4/N$, implying $NW\approx 4$, but the reader should not have to infer the design parameter from pixel measurement.
  - [ADVISORY] Titles report "width 5.8/N" etc. without saying which width; only the caption discloses that this is the half-power (3 dB) width. Panel-title and caption terminology should match.
  - [ADVISORY] Traces in (m) and (n) exit the bottom of the axis, so the plotted dynamic range is clipped and the true sidelobe floor is unreadable for those two columns while it is readable for (o) and (p) (~−30 and ~−38 dB). This creates an unintended asymmetry in what the reader can compare across columns.
  - [ADVISORY] Panels (a)–(h) have a y-axis label but no ticks or scale, with arbitrary vertical offsets. Acceptable as a stacked-trace convention, but must be declared so amplitude is not over-read; note also that whether each taper is unit-norm is not conveyed.
  - [ADVISORY] Legend/label collisions: "k = 1" over the $k=0$ curve in (d); panel letters (b), (c), (d) colliding with the multi-line column titles.
  - [ADVISORY] The number of retained tapers differs by column (7, ~12, ~10, 7) but is stated in the title only for column (d), so the $\nu$ values in (i)–(l) are compared without the $K$ that produces them being uniformly visible.
  - [ADVISORY] $\nu$ is presented as a bare figure of merit across columns whose resolution bandwidths differ by ~40%, inviting a one-dimensional "best" reading of a two-dimensional variance/resolution trade-off.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - State $N$, $W$ (or $NW$), and the sampling convention once in the caption, e.g. "$N = 256$, $NW = 4$; frequency in units of $1/N$."
  - Add $K$ to the title or the eigen-weight panel of every column, not just column (d); or annotate each of (i)–(l) with "$K = \cdot$, $\nu = \cdot$."
  - Either extend the y-limits of (m)–(p) until all four traces are fully contained, or keep the −80 dB floor and add a note that traces are clipped at the floor. Keeping the limits identical across the four panels is correct and should be retained either way.
  - Change the panel titles to "half-power (3 dB) width = 5.8/N" to match the caption's terminology.
  - Add to the caption: "In (a)–(h) curves are vertically offset for display; the amplitude axis is arbitrary and traces are [unit-norm / not normalized]."
  - Reposition the (d) legend (or move it outside the axes) and nudge the (b), (c), (d) panel letters clear of the column titles; consider placing panel letters inside the axes at upper-left for the whole figure for consistency.
  - Increase line width or downsample/anti-alias the (p) sidelobe structure, and check rendering at final column width.
  - Add one sentence to the caption noting that $\nu$ and kernel width trade off, so columns should be compared as (resolution, variance) pairs rather than ranked by $\nu$ alone.
  - No error bars, confidence bands, or individual-point overlays should be added to (i)–(l): these are exact deterministic weights, and adding dispersion indicators would misrepresent them.

### figure1.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — This is a methodological/deterministic demonstration (spectral estimates of a single record), not a group-comparison dataset, so the bar-graph/distribution-hiding concerns do not apply. Line plots for PSD vs. frequency (a, b) and a log–log line plot for the scaling relation (c) are the correct choices. One design caveat: in (a) the "agreement" is partly *created* by the thick grey multitaper trace, whose linewidth spans roughly 1 dB of ordinate; any discrepancy smaller than the stroke width is invisible by construction. Panel (b) is what carries the evidence, so this is acceptable but should be acknowledged rather than presented as an independent visual check.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — $N=1024$ samples and "one epoch, one channel" appear only in the caption, not in the figure. Because each panel is a single deterministic realisation, there are no replicate data points to show; but the reader cannot tell from the graphic that $n_{\text{epochs}} = n_{\text{channels}} = 1$. Panel (c) shows 5 points, each presumably a single computation with no repetition across records.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — The three routes are computed from the *same* record, i.e. these are inherently paired comparisons, and they are correctly visualised as such: overlaid on a common frequency axis in (a) and as pointwise differences in (b). This is the right treatment of paired data. Minor concern: the numerical claims in (b) ("$3\times10^{-13}$ dB", "0.06 dB") are maxima over frequency that are hard to read off the plot; the pink trace's peaks appear to top out somewhere between $10^{-2}$ and $10^{-1}$, which is consistent with 0.06 dB and with the $L/N=16$ point in (c), but authors should verify that the stated maxima are the actual plotted maxima and not values from a different run.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None (not misleading) — (a) begins near ~15 dB rather than zero, but decibels are a logarithmic ratio scale for which a zero baseline is meaningless, and no group difference is being visually inflated. (b) and (c) are logarithmic by necessity; the 15-decade range in (b) is honest and appropriate. Panel (c)'s abscissa is log-spaced with powers-of-two ticks, correctly representing uneven intervals.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (rendering-level) — Panels (a) and (b) plot on the order of $10^3$ frequency bins compressed into ~800 px. At this density the raster rendering can drop or flatten isolated extrema, which matters here because the scientific claim is about a *maximum* absolute difference. The narrow downward spikes in the green and pink traces of (b) also cannot be resolved individually. This does not bias the conclusion (the stated maxima come from the data, not the pixels), but a reader cannot verify the maxima visually.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential comparisons or significance annotations are present; the differences shown are exact numerical discrepancies, not test statistics.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — Both x-axes are frequency and window length; there is no longitudinal interpolation across missing timepoints. The powers-of-two window lengths in (c) are joined by straight segments on log axes, which is a legitimate guide-to-the-eye between computed values, not interpolation across missing data.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] Sample/record provenance is invisible in the figure: a reader sees three curves with no indication that all panels derive from a single 8-s epoch of a single EEG channel. Since the agreement being demonstrated is (per the paper's own argument) an algebraic identity rather than an empirical average, this is defensible, but the graphic overstates its own generality.
- [ADVISORY] The thick grey reference trace in (a) has a stroke width large enough to conceal deviations of order 1 dB, i.e. more than an order of magnitude larger than the largest discrepancy reported in (b). Panel (a) therefore cannot fail, and should not be described as evidence of agreement independent of (b).
- [ADVISORY] Panel (b)'s reported maxima ($3\times10^{-13}$ dB; 0.06 dB) are not readable from the plot at this line density; overplotting means the visually apparent envelope may not coincide with the true maximum.
- [ADVISORY] Panel (c) shows five single-run points with no indication of variability across records or realisations. The deviation from the $1/L$ guide at $L/N=2$ and the drop below it at $L/N=16$ are uninterpretable without knowing whether they are reproducible or record-specific.
- [ADVISORY] Colour/description mismatch: the caption calls the third trace "dashed purple" while it renders as a pink/mauve and is labelled "average" in the legend. Green-vs-pink is discriminable for most common colour-vision deficiencies, but the pairing is not ideal for deuteranopia at thin linewidths.
- [ADVISORY] The equivalent degrees of freedom ($\nu=17$) and the half-bandwidth $W$ are stated in the caption only; neither $W$ nor $\nu$ is marked on any axis, so the smoothing scale cannot be judged against the spectral peak widths in (a).

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Annotate $n$ and provenance directly in the figure, e.g. "one channel, one 8-s epoch, $N=1024$, $W=0.5$ Hz" as a small in-panel text block in (a).
- Reduce the grey multitaper linewidth to a value comparable to the other traces (or plot it as a thin solid line with the other two as dashed/dotted overlays), so that panel (a) is capable of revealing disagreement rather than guaranteed to hide it.
- Mark the reported maxima in (b) explicitly: add horizontal dashed reference lines at $3\times10^{-13}$ dB and $0.06$ dB with inline labels, so the quoted numbers are verifiable from the graphic.
- Mitigate the rendering-level overplotting in (a) and (b): either widen the panels, use a max-envelope decimation that provably preserves extrema, or supply the figure as vector output at sufficient resolution.
- In (c), either state in the caption that each point is a single deterministic computation, or (preferably) repeat the calculation across several epochs/channels and plot the individual points (5 lengths × $k$ records, with jitter if needed) so the reader can see whether the $1/L$ trend and its deviations are robust.
- Add a horizontal bar or bracket of width $2W$ in panel (a) to make the smoothing bandwidth visually comparable to the peak widths, and report $\nu$ in-panel.
- Reconcile the caption's colour words with the rendered colours, and consider adding a redundant encoding (marker or dash pattern differences already partly present) so the three routes remain distinguishable in greyscale.

### figure2.png

---
**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Line/curve overlays are the correct display for PSD estimates and kernel responses; no bar charts, pie charts or error-bar summaries are involved, so items 1 and 2 of the statistical-visualization checklist largely do not apply. Two minor plot-type remarks: (i) in (e) the Fejér kernel is rendered as a dense grey mass rather than a curve, so it reads as a filled area and competes visually with the grey "true spectrum (shape only)" band drawn in the *same* grey — consider plotting the kernel as a thin dark line and the true-spectrum shape as a light fill; (ii) panels (c),(d) plot the smoothed estimate as a continuous curve, which is fine, but the underlying frequency grid spacing should be stated so readers know the curve is not interpolated across a coarser smoothed grid.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — $N=1024$ appears only in the caption, never in the figure. More importantly for this figure, the *number of realizations is one*, and the quantity the figure is arguing about (estimator variance, summarized by $\nu$) is precisely the quantity a single realization cannot display. A reader cannot tell from the figure whether the visual "noisiness" differences in (a)–(d) are representative or a favorable draw. Annotate $N=1024$ in-panel (e.g., in (a)), and either (a) overlay a pointwise 95% interval from the known $\chi^2_\nu$ distribution, or (b) add faint traces from several independent realizations behind the highlighted one.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — This is intrinsically *paired* data: the four estimators in (a)–(d) are only comparable if they are computed from the *same* realization of the AR process. The caption's "One realization ($N=1024$) ... estimated by the periodogram (a), a Hann-tapered periodogram (b), ..." implies this but does not state it unambiguously, and nothing in the figure establishes it. If different draws (or different seeds) were used, the apparent noise-reduction from (a)→(c) is confounded with realization-to-realization variability. State explicitly "the same realization in all panels (seed fixed)". Similarly, the paired nature of (a)/(e), (b)/(f), etc., is implied only by column position; add a shared column label or matching colour/edge cue so the vertical pairing is unambiguous.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — Two instances, one of which is substantive.
1. (e)–(h) are truncated at $-80$ dB. The headline annotation in these panels is the fraction of kernel power "beyond $2W$", which depends on kernel behaviour in exactly the region that is clipped. The claim "$<0.001\%$ beyond $2W$" in (f)/(g) is therefore not visually verifiable, and the contrast with (e)'s "1.3%" is asserted rather than shown. This is not a *misleading exaggeration* of a difference, but it is an unsupported-by-the-graphic quantitative claim.
2. (b) appears to clip the estimate at the bottom axis limit near $f\approx0.42$ (the trace runs into the $-40$-ish boundary), so the true excursion depth is hidden. Because the visual argument in (b) is "still noisy", clipping the largest downward excursions understates the very quantity being illustrated.
Shared y-limits across (a)–(d) and across (e)–(h) are good practice and should be stated in the caption.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present — Panel (a) is saturated: the raw periodogram at $N=1024$ produces ~512 densely packed vertical excursions that merge into a solid grey band, so the reader can see the *envelope* but not the distribution of values within it (the $\chi^2_2$ scatter, the noise floor level, or whether the "floor" is a true floor or a dense cloud). The legend text "estimate (one realization)" additionally overlaps the data trace in (a), and the grey legend swatches for "peak width 7/N" / "separation 30/N" sit inside the data region. Panel (b) is similarly dense in its right half. Suggest reduced line width plus transparency, a thinned/decimated display, or an inset zoom over a narrow frequency band showing the individual periodogram ordinates.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No inferential annotations, p-values, or pairwise significance markers appear; nothing requires correction. ($\nu$ is a descriptive property of each estimator, not a test statistic.)

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — Axes are frequency, not time; no gaps, uneven intervals, or interpolation across missing epochs. (Note for the authors: because the abscissa is a frequency grid, any resampling/decimation used for plotting should be disclosed so readers do not mistake plotted resolution for estimator resolution.)

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] Caption–figure inconsistency in the annotation legend: the caption states "The short blue bar above the peaks is the half-power width of each estimator's kernel", implying it appears in every top panel, but blue bars appear only in (c) and (d). Likewise the caption says "the grey bars in (a) mark the peak width and separation", but in the rendered figure these grey elements read as *legend swatches* rather than as bars positioned on the frequency axis at the stated width/separation. As drawn, the key quantitative scales (7/N, 30/N) cannot be read off the data. Either add the bars to (a)/(b) and place the 7/N and 30/N markers on the axis at their actual locations, or reword the caption to describe only what is drawn.
- [BLOCKING] Single realization used to support a claim about estimator variance, with no uncertainty display and no in-figure $n$. The visual difference in "noisiness" between panels is not distinguishable from draw-to-draw luck as presented.
- [ADVISORY] Apparent clipping of the (b) trace at the lower y-limit; excursion depth hidden in the panel whose message is "still noisy".
- [ADVISORY] $-80$ dB floor in (e)–(h) hides the kernel tails that the "% beyond $2W$" annotations quantify.
- [ADVISORY] Severe overplotting in (a) (and the right half of b); legend text overlaps data in (a).
- [ADVISORY] Panel-letter collisions: the (g) and (h) labels overlap their panel titles ("(g)kernel: ...", "(h) kernel: ..."), and the (a) label sits adjacent to the legend box. Reduces legibility at print size.
- [ADVISORY] Grey is used for three distinct meanings across the figure (the periodogram estimate in a; the Fejér kernel in e; the "true spectrum, shape only" band in e–h). Orange is used for both estimates (b–d) and kernels (f–h). The colour mapping is not one-to-one, which forces the reader back to the caption.
- [ADVISORY] Pairing between the top and bottom rows is conveyed only by horizontal position; no explicit cue links (a)↔(e), (b)↔(f), etc.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- State in the caption that all four estimates come from the *same* realization with a fixed seed, and report the seed or make the generating script available; this converts (a)–(d) from an implicitly to an explicitly paired comparison.
- Add a light pointwise 95% band from the analytic $\chi^2_\nu$ sampling distribution (or a faint ensemble of ~20 independent realizations) behind each estimate in (a)–(d). This shows, rather than asserts, the variance reduction tracked by $\nu$, and it makes the single highlighted trace interpretable.
- Print $N=1024$ and the number of realizations inside panel (a); print $\nu$ values as they already are (good) and keep them in-panel.
- Fix the clipping in (b): extend the lower y-limit until no excursion touches the axis, or annotate the clipped minimum explicitly. Keep the limits shared across (a)–(d) and say so in the caption.
- For (e)–(h), replace or supplement the "% beyond $2W$" text with a visual that survives the $-80$ dB truncation — e.g., a small inset showing the cumulative fraction of kernel power as a function of $|f|$, with $W$ and $2W$ marked. Alternatively extend the y-limit and note the floor explicitly ("curves clipped at $-80$ dB").
- Draw the 7/N peak-width and 30/N separation markers *on the frequency axis of (a) at their true positions* (e.g., a horizontal span under the peaks with tick ends), not only as legend swatches; repeat them faintly in (b)–(d) so the reader can compare each kernel's blue half-power bar against the same reference in every panel. Add the blue half-power bar to (a) and (b) as well, or restrict the caption's wording to (c)–(d).
- Reduce overplotting in (a)/(b): line width ≤0.5 pt with alpha ≈0.5, and add an inset zoom over a ~0.05-wide frequency band where individual periodogram ordinates are resolvable.
- Rationalize the colour scheme: one colour for estimates, a distinct colour for kernels, and a neutral light fill used *only* for the true-spectrum shape. Add a single shared legend outside the axes rather than a legend inside (a) and another inside (e).
- Nudge the (g) and (h) panel letters left/up (or move titles down) to eliminate the label–title overlap, and move the (a) legend outside the data region.
- Add a column cue (shared column titles, or matching axis-spine colour for each estimator) so the (a)↔(e) … (d)↔(h) pairing is visible without reading the caption.
- Caption mechanics: define "half-power width" and "equivalent degrees of freedom" on first use or cite the defining equation; confirm whether $W$ is a half-width throughout (the text says "half-width $W=4/N$" and then "$2W$ equals the width of a peak", which is consistent, but the panel titles read "box $W = 4/N$" without "half-", so add "half-" to the titles for internal consistency).
---

### figure3.png

---

**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Neither panel is a categorical summary plot, so the bar-graph/pie-chart concerns do not apply. Panel (a) is a raw trace (the strongest possible form of data display: every sample is shown). Panel (b) is a line plot of a Monte-Carlo-averaged error functional against a tuning parameter, which is the conventional and correct form for a bias–variance tradeoff curve. One residual risk: panel (b) reduces 300 realizations per value of $W$ to a single point, so the *distribution* of the per-realization RMS error is entirely hidden. A shaded band (e.g. 10th–90th percentile across realizations, or mean ± Monte-Carlo standard error) would restore the information without changing the figure's structure.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Visible — $N = 1024$ samples is stated in the panel (a) title; "300 realizations" is stated in the panel (b) title. Both are inferable from the figure alone without the caption, which is good practice. Individual realizations are not shown in (b), but with $n = 300$ the "always show individual points" rule does not bind; an aggregate spread indicator is the appropriate substitute.

**COMPARISON:** Valid, with one flag — The solid (whole band) and dashed (peak region) curves are presumably computed on the *same* 300 realizations, i.e. they are paired by realization. This is not stated anywhere. If the two curves come from the same ensemble, saying so strengthens the claim that the peak-region minimum sits at genuinely smaller $W$, because the shift is then within-realization rather than between-ensemble. Separately, the circled "just right, $\nu = 9$" marker sits near $W \approx 4$, which does not coincide with the minimum of either black curve (the solid minimum appears to lie near $W \approx 8$–9, the dashed near $W \approx 5$). Authors should verify that the circled point is the one intended and, if so, explain in the caption why the chosen operating point is deliberately to the left of the whole-band minimum.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** None — Panel (b) ordinate includes zero and the bias curve is visibly anchored at 0 dB, so the relative magnitudes of noise, bias and total are read honestly. Panel (a) ordinate is symmetric about zero, appropriate for a zero-mean process. No exaggeration of differences by truncation in either panel.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (mild, both panels) — In panel (a), 1024 samples of a narrowband oscillatory process are drawn into roughly 1000 px, so the trace is at the rendering limit; the grey untapered record is largely overdrawn by the orange tapered record in the central third, and fine amplitude structure becomes solid ink. This is tolerable because the panel's message is the envelope, not individual cycles, but an inset zoom over ~100 samples would let a reader verify that the orange curve really is the grey curve times the dashed envelope. In panel (b) the marker sampling in $W$ is strongly uneven (dense at $W \lesssim 6$, sparse beyond $W \approx 12$, with gaps of 4 units), and straight-line interpolation across those gaps could conceal curvature or a secondary minimum. The markers do disclose where data exist, which is the right choice; the caption should nevertheless state that the curves are linear interpolation between computed grid points.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No pairwise tests, $p$-values or significance brackets are annotated anywhere in the figure, so no correction question arises.

**TIME SERIES:** OK — Panel (a) is a contiguous, evenly sampled record with no missing timepoints and no interpolation across gaps; the abscissa runs 0 to 1024 in sample index, matching the stated $N$. Panel (b) is not a time series.

**ISSUES:**
- [ADVISORY] The caption's stated reason for omitting uncertainty — "no error bars are shown because the curves are ensemble averages" — is not a valid justification. An ensemble average over 300 realizations has a Monte-Carlo standard error of its own, and the spread of the underlying per-realization RMS errors is a scientifically relevant quantity (it tells the reader how reliably a *single* record would land near the optimum). The correct statement is either "Monte-Carlo standard errors are smaller than the line width" (if verified) or a displayed band.
- [ADVISORY] Distribution across the 300 realizations is not shown in panel (b); only the mean functional is visible. A reader cannot tell whether the broad minimum is broad in the ensemble mean only or also stable realization by realization.
- [ADVISORY] The pairing of the solid and dashed curves (same realizations or independent ensembles?) is unstated, so the reader cannot judge whether the leftward shift of the peak-region optimum is a within-ensemble effect.
- [ADVISORY] The circled "just right, $\nu=9$" operating point does not coincide with the minimum of either error curve; this may be intentional but currently reads as an inconsistency.
- [ADVISORY] Uneven grid spacing in $W$ with linear interpolation across gaps of up to 4 units in the large-$W$ region; possible hidden structure is not excluded by the display.
- [ADVISORY] The two black curves are distinguished only by dash pattern and both use black markers; in small print or greyscale reproduction they are easy to conflate, and the orange annotation circles sit on top of them.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Add a translucent band to the total-error curves in panel (b) showing either mean ± Monte-Carlo standard error or the 10th–90th percentile of per-realization RMS error, and amend the caption to report which. If the standard error is genuinely thinner than the stroke, say so explicitly rather than asserting that averages need no uncertainty.
- Replace the caption clause "no error bars are shown because the curves are ensemble averages" with a statement of the actual uncertainty, e.g. "the Monte-Carlo standard error of each curve is below 0.05 dB, smaller than the line width."
- State in the caption whether the whole-band and peak-region curves are evaluated on the same 300 realizations.
- Verify the placement of the "just right, $\nu=9$" circle against the curve minima; if the offset is deliberate, add a clause explaining the choice (e.g. that the peak-region criterion governs the selection).
- Note in the caption that the curves are linear interpolants between the plotted grid points, or densify the grid for $W > 12$.
- Distinguish the two black curves more strongly (e.g. different marker shapes, or a mid-grey for the peak-region curve) and consider a small inset in panel (a) zooming ~100 samples to demonstrate the taper multiplication directly.

### figure4.png

---

**Context note:** This figure presents deterministic, analytically/numerically generated quantities (Slepian tapers, spectral concentration windows, eigenvalue ladder) for a single fixed parameter set (*N* = 256, *W* = 4/*N*). There is no sampled empirical data, no biological/experimental replication, and no inferential statistics. Most items on the statistical-visualization checklist are therefore **not applicable**; they are marked as such rather than forced. The review below concentrates on the items that do apply (axis integrity, overplotting/legibility, interpolation across discrete indices) plus one figure–caption consistency concern.

---
PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots for continuous functions of *t* and *f* (a–c) and a marker+line plot for a discrete eigenvalue sequence (d) are the correct choices. No bar charts, no pie charts, no error bars; nothing here is a summary statistic that could hide a distribution. The vertical-offset stacking in (a) is a standard and honest convention for shape comparison, and the caption discloses it ("offset for display") as does the axis label ("taper (offset)").

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not applicable / adequately specified — There is no *n* in the statistical sense. The relevant "size" parameters (*N* = 256, *W* = 4/*N*, *K* = 2*NW* − 1 = 7) are given in the caption and partly inferable from the figure: panel (a) spans *t* = 0–255, panel (d) marks 2*NW* = 8 and *K* = 7 with annotated guide lines. Every element of the discrete sequence in (d) is plotted as an individual marker (17 points, *k* = 0–16), which is the correct practice for a small discrete set. **Advisory:** *N* and *W* appear only in the caption; consider an in-panel annotation (e.g. "*N* = 256, *NW* = 4") so each panel is self-contained when reproduced in slides.

COMPARISON:         Valid, with one consistency concern — The nested-subset comparison in (c) ("first 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, all *N*") is the appropriate paired/nested presentation: all curves share one axis and one normalization (unit area), so the progressive filling of the band is directly readable. The concern is not the visualization but the caption claim: the black "all *N* tapers" curve in (c) is drawn with visibly **rounded shoulders and a finite-width transition** at ±4, yet the caption states it "is *exactly* the box of half-width *W* (dashed)". As plotted, the black curve departs from the dashed ideal box near the band edges. Either (i) the identity holds only in a limiting/smoothed sense on the plotted frequency grid, (ii) the curve is not the full-*N* sum, or (iii) the discrepancy is a plotting-resolution artifact. This must be resolved so the visual and the text agree.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None misleading — Panels (b) and (c) both include *y* = 0 in the visible range, so no exaggeration of peak heights. Panel (a) has no *y* tick labels, which is acceptable and in fact preferable given the arbitrary vertical offsets (a labeled axis there would be misleading). Panel (d) uses a base-10 log *y* axis spanning 10⁻¹⁶ to ~10⁰; this is appropriate and necessary for a quantity ranging over fifteen decades, and the log scaling is unambiguous from the tick labels. **Advisory:** a log axis with a floor near machine epsilon (10⁻¹⁶) can visually overstate the reliability of the smallest values (*k* = 0 at ~10⁻¹⁰, and any point pushed toward the floor). State in the caption that values below roughly 10⁻¹⁴ are at the limit of double-precision eigenvalue computation and should not be read quantitatively.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present (legibility, not density) — Two distinct problems:
(1) **Legend/axis-label collision.** In panel (a) the legend row overlaps the *x*-axis label "sample t"; in panel (b) the legend overlaps "frequency offset (units of 1/N)". The label text and legend glyphs are interleaved and partially illegible. This is a layout defect, not a data defect, but it degrades the figure at print size.
(2) **Curve congestion in (b).** Eight sequential-colormap (viridis) curves with many oscillatory sidelobes overlap heavily inside |*f*| ≤ *W*. Distinguishing *k* = 3 from *k* = 4 near the band center requires close inspection, and the lower-amplitude sidelobes of high-*k* windows are buried under neighbors.
Panel (d) has no overplotting — 17 markers are cleanly separated.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: Not applicable — No hypothesis tests, *p*-values, or significance annotations appear anywhere in the figure. Nothing to correct.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable (a–c) / minor concern (d) — Panel (a) is indexed by sample *t* but is a deterministic basis function on a complete, evenly spaced grid, so there are no gaps and no interpolation risk. Panel (d) connects integer taper indices *k* with a continuous line. There is no quantity defined between *k* = 3 and *k* = 4, so the connecting line is decorative interpolation across a discrete domain. The markers make the true support clear, so this is low-severity, but a stem plot or markers-only presentation would be strictly more honest.

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] Figure–caption contradiction in panel (c): the caption asserts the all-*N*-taper sum is "*exactly* the box," but the plotted black curve has rounded shoulders and a finite transition width at ±4 and does not coincide with the dashed ideal box. As drawn, the figure contradicts the stated identity. Verify what is actually being summed and normalized, and reconcile text with the plot.
  - [BLOCKING] Legend text overlaps the *x*-axis labels in panels (a) and (b) ("sample t" and "frequency offset (units of 1/N)" are obscured). Both axis labels are rendered unreadable at publication size.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (b) legend lists *k* = 0–7 while panel (a) shows only *k* = 0–5; the caption explains this ("first six... their spectral windows"), but the mismatch between "their" and eight plotted windows is confusing on first reading.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (d) draws a continuous line through a strictly discrete index *k*, implying values between integers that do not exist.
  - [ADVISORY] Panel (d) log axis extends to 10⁻¹⁶, within numerical-precision range; the smallest 1 − λ_k values may be computation-limited rather than meaningful.
  - [ADVISORY] Eight overlapping viridis curves in (b) are hard to separate, particularly adjacent *k* values in the band interior.
  - [ADVISORY] *N*, *W*, and *K* live only in the caption; individual panels are not self-contained if extracted.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Resolve the (c) discrepancy: if the identity Σ_k λ_k|U_k(f)|²/2NW = box holds only after averaging over the Fourier grid or in the *N* → ∞ limit, soften the caption ("approaches the box to within plotting accuracy; the edge transition is the finite-*N* residual") and, ideally, add a thin inset showing the black-minus-dashed residual near *f* = ±4. If the curve is truly the exact box, increase the frequency sampling so the vertical edges render and the black curve visually coincides with the dashed line.
  - Fix layout: move all legends outside the axes (e.g. `bbox_to_anchor` to the right of each panel, or a single shared figure-level legend for *k* since (a) and (b) share the colormap), or reserve space with `constrained_layout` / increased `bottom` margin so axis labels are never overdrawn.
  - Replace the per-panel *k* legends in (a) and (b) with one shared horizontal colorbar labeled "taper index *k*", which removes ~12 legend entries, frees the space causing the collisions, and makes the sequential ordering of the viridis colors explicit.
  - In (b), either reduce to the same six tapers shown in (a) for a one-to-one correspondence, or keep eight and reword the caption to "spectral windows for the first eight tapers." Optionally add small in-band labels at each window's peak so curves are identified without legend lookup.
  - In (d), switch to a stem/marker-only plot, or keep the line but lighten it (thin, 40% alpha) so the markers clearly carry the data. Add a horizontal reference line at the leakage plateau and label it.
  - In (d), clip or grey-shade the region below ~10⁻¹⁴ and note "below double-precision resolution" in the caption.
  - Add compact in-panel parameter annotations (e.g. "*N* = 256, *NW* = 4" in (a); "*K* = 7" beside the grey guide line in (d)) so panels stand alone.
  - Confirm the grey band shading in (b) and the dashed box in (c) use identical edge positions (±4 in units of 1/*N*) and state in the caption that both mark |*f*| ≤ *W*, so readers can align the two panels.
  - Since all curves are deterministic, add one sentence to the caption stating that no error bars or variability estimates are shown because the quantities are computed exactly (up to floating-point precision) from the DPSS eigenproblem — this preempts a reader assuming omitted uncertainty.
  - For reproducibility, report the DPSS routine and tolerance used (e.g. `scipy.signal.windows.dpss(256, 4, Kmax=...)`) and the frequency grid density used for panels (b) and (c), since the apparent edge sharpness in (c) depends directly on the latter.
---

### figure5.png

---
PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — This is a methods/illustration figure showing deterministic mathematical objects (window functions in a–d, normalized eigen-weight ladders in e–h), not sampled empirical data. Bar charts in (e–h) are the correct choice for a discrete, indexed, non-random quantity $c_k$; the usual objection to bar-plus-error-bar graphs (hidden distributions) does not apply because there is no distribution — there is one exact value per $k$. No error bars are shown, which is correct. Line plots in (a–d) are appropriate for tapers. One note: the tapers are discrete sequences of length $N=256$ drawn as continuous curves; at this resolution that is visually harmless, but the caption or axis label should confirm that no smoothing/interpolation beyond sample-to-sample joining was applied.

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — $N=256$ and $W=4/N$ appear only in the caption, not on the figure. Panel titles do carry the structurally important counts ($L=N/4$, 50% overlap, $\sigma=3/N$, $K=7$), and the number of bars in (e–h) makes the effective ladder length $K$ directly readable, which is good practice. Since these are exact deterministic values, "individual data points" are already fully shown in e–h (one bar = one exact value). Recommend adding "$N=256$, $W=4/N$" as an on-figure annotation so each panel is self-contained when reproduced in slides.

COMPARISON:         Concern — The four banks are meant to be compared side by side, and two things could mislead a reader:
(i) Because $c_k$ is normalized to unit sum, bar *height* is inversely tied to the number of retained tapers. Panel (f) looks "taller" than (h) not because any individual taper carries more absolute weight in a meaningful sense, but because the ladder is normalized. The correct comparison quantity is ladder *flatness* ($\nu$), which is annotated in each title — good — but the visual impression of the bars invites the wrong reading.
(ii) Panel (f) represents a continuum of frequency-shifted copies truncated to ~12 bars, and panel (g) a limit process ("every offset") truncated to ~10 bars. The truncation rule (weight threshold? fixed $K$?) is not stated, yet $\nu$ depends on it. Two panels are exact finite banks (a/e with 7, d/h with 7) and two are truncations of infinite/continuum banks — that asymmetry should be made explicit.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    None — All four bottom panels start at $y=0$ and share identical limits (0 to ~0.45) and identical $x$ ranges (to ~12), which is exactly right and makes cross-panel comparison honest. Top panels (a–d) deliberately omit a numeric $y$ scale because curves are vertically offset; the axis label says "amplitude (curves offset)", which is adequate disclosure, though stating that offsets are arbitrary and equal would be better.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — Not data-density overplotting, but annotation overplotting that obscures curves:
- Panel labels **(c)** and **(d)** collide with the third and fourth column titles ("bank: a sinc window..." and "bank: Slepian tapers k = 0..3"); (b) is also crowded.
- The in-axes legends in (b), (c) and (d) sit directly on top of the $k=0$ and $k=1$ traces. In (d) the legend visibly cuts through the $k=0$ and $k=1$ curves, and in (c) it obscures the top trace's oscillation structure near $t\approx150$–250.
- In (c) the green ($k=3$) trace rises steeply at $t\gtrsim230$ and appears clipped at the panel boundary; if the curve exceeds the drawn range the reader cannot tell the true excursion.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: No concern — No inferential comparisons, no $p$-values or significance brackets. Nothing to correct.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — The $x$ axis in (a–d) is sample index of a fixed-length record with uniform spacing and no gaps; there is no longitudinal/missing-timepoint issue. In (a) the flat-zero regions outside each Hann segment are genuine zeros, not missing data — worth one clause in the caption so a reader does not mistake them for gaps.

ISSUES:
  - [ADVISORY] Panel labels (b), (c), (d) overlap the column titles, and the legends in (b)–(d) overlay the top one or two curves. This is a legibility/data-obscuring defect that will worsen at print column width.
  - [ADVISORY] Possible clipping of the $k=3$ curve at the right edge of panel (c); verify the full excursion is within the plotted range or extend the $y$ limits.
  - [ADVISORY] Ladders in (f) and (g) are truncations of a continuum/limit bank, but the truncation criterion is unstated even though the reported $\nu$ depends on it. As drawn, (e)/(h) (exact finite banks) and (f)/(g) (truncated) look like the same kind of object.
  - [ADVISORY] Unit-sum normalization makes bar heights across panels a function of $K$ rather than of any physically comparable weight; readers may over-interpret the height difference between (f) and (h).
  - [ADVISORY] $N=256$ and $W=4/N$ — the parameters that fix every curve in the figure — are caption-only, so the figure is not self-contained.
  - [ADVISORY] No numeric $y$ scale in (a–d); vertical offsets are undeclared in magnitude, so relative amplitudes between tapers cannot be judged.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Move panel letters (a)–(h) outside the axes (e.g. upper-left of each subplot bounding box, or use a consistent negative-offset transform) and shorten or wrap the column titles so letters and titles no longer collide.
  - Move the legends in (b)–(d) outside the axes (single shared legend below the top row, or to the right of column 4), or place them in an empty region; do not overlay traces.
  - Check and extend $y$ limits in (c) so no curve is clipped; alternatively note in the caption that the $k=3$ eigen-taper diverges at the record edge.
  - State the truncation rule for (f) and (g) explicitly in the caption (e.g. "ladder truncated at $c_k < 10^{-3}$; $\nu$ computed from the full ladder") and consider marking truncated ladders with an ellipsis or open-ended bar to distinguish them from the exact finite banks in (e) and (h).
  - Add on-figure annotation "$N=256$, $W=4/N$" (e.g. as a figure supertitle or in one panel corner).
  - Add a scale bar or a stated uniform offset value to (a–d), e.g. "curves offset by 1.0; all tapers unit-normalized", so relative amplitudes are recoverable.
  - Optionally overlay the reference flat ladder (panel h) as a light outline in (e)–(g) so the flatness comparison that drives $\nu$ is visual rather than only numeric.
  - Confirm in the caption that (a–d) plot the discrete sequences joined sample-to-sample with no additional smoothing.

### figure6.png

---

**Context note:** This is a methods/simulation figure (spectral estimators, frequency-domain line plots), not a group-comparison figure. Several checklist items (bar-vs-dot plots, pie charts, pairwise annotation) are structurally not applicable. The items that *do* bite here are sample-size visibility (n = 1 realization supporting quantitative claims), axis clipping, and an un-calibrated overlay.

---

PLOT TYPE:          Appropriate — Line plots over a continuous frequency axis are the correct encoding for PSD estimates and kernels. No bar/pie encodings; no distributional summaries are being substituted for raw data. Filled gray region in (e–h) is a reasonable background reference *provided* it is calibrated (see ISSUES).

SAMPLE SIZE:        Not visible — $N=1024$, $K=7$, 9 Welch segments, and $W=4/N$ appear only in the caption/titles, not as in-panel annotations for every subplot. More importantly, the effective replicate count is **one realization**, and this is stated only in the caption. The equivalent degrees of freedom ($\nu = 17.2, 14.0, 8.9, 17.6$) are commendably shown in the panel titles — this is good practice and is the closest thing to an in-figure n. But $\nu$ describes the smoothing bandwidth, not the number of independent Monte Carlo draws, and a reader may conflate the two.

COMPARISON:         Concern — Two separate issues:
 (i) *Favorable pairing, but unexploited.* All four estimators are applied to the **same** record, so this is a paired design. That is the right experimental choice, but the figure presents the four results as four unlinked panels. Nothing lets the reader see the correlated structure (e.g., that the same realization noise drives the same wiggles in b, c, d).
 (ii) *Unequal $\nu$ undermines the error ranking.* The median-absolute-error values quoted in the caption (11.6 / 4.6 / 1.3 / 0.8 dB) are compared across estimators whose degrees of freedom differ by a factor of two ($\nu = 8.9$ for Hann vs 17.6 for Welch). Median absolute error mixes bias and variance, and variance scales as $\approx 1/\nu$. Panel (c) is thus penalized on variance relative to (d) despite the caption framing the comparison as bias/leakage. The half-power widths are matched; the variances are not.

AXIS TRUNCATION:    Present — Not the classic misleading bar-chart truncation, but two genuine clipping problems:
 - Top row (a–d) shares a common $-40$ to $\sim$45 dB range: **good**, cross-panel comparison is fair.
 - Bottom row (e–h) is clipped at $-80$ dB. The Hann kernel in (g) plunges off the bottom of the axis near $\pm 18/N$, and the Welch kernel in (h) is clipped at its nulls. The caption's headline claim for (g) — "$< 0.001\%$ beyond $2W$", "falls below $-36$ dB" — cannot be visually verified because the very region that carries the claim is below the axis floor.
 - Bottom row x-range is truncated to $\pm 40/N$. The "% beyond $2W$" statistic is an integral over *all* offsets, including the far tails that are outside the plotted window. The Fejér tail in (e) is still at $\approx -36$ dB at the plot edge and is clearly not yet decayed, so the reader cannot see what dominates the 1.4% figure.
 - The blue trace in (b) and orange in (c) appear to stop near $f \approx 0.45$ while the green in (a) and purple in (d) extend to 0.5. If this is an end-effect/segment artifact it should be stated; if it is a plotting truncation it should be fixed, since unequal plotted support subtly changes the apparent noise-floor comparison.

OVERPLOTTING:       Present — (b) and (f) contain high-frequency oscillatory structure (per-bin noise, Slepian sidelobe ripple) rendered at a line density approaching the pixel pitch. At 2094 px across four panels this will alias/moiré in print and at reduced figure width, and the apparent sidelobe "floor" level in (f) becomes a function of rendering rather than of the data. The same applies to the dense noise floor in (b), where the visual center of the band is not the true mean.

MULTIPLE COMPARISONS: Flag for verification — No p-values or significance brackets are annotated, so no correction is formally required. However, the figure plus caption effectively asserts a **four-way ranking of estimator performance** on the basis of a single realization. That is a multiple-comparison-adjacent claim: with $n = 1$ draw and $\nu \approx 9$–18, the sampling scatter of a median absolute error statistic is large enough that the ordering of the two best performers (1.3 vs 0.8 dB) may not be stable. Authors should verify the ranking over an ensemble.

TIME SERIES:        Not applicable — Frequency-domain display. No temporal gaps or uneven sampling are implicated. (If the underlying record in Fig. \ref{fig:motif} has any missing samples, that must be disclosed there, since all four estimators assume a gap-free record.)

ISSUES:
  - [BLOCKING] Quantitative performance claims (median absolute error 11.6 / 4.6 / 1.3 / 0.8 dB; "floors 20 dB high"; "floors 10 dB high") are derived from a **single realization** and are presented without any uncertainty. These are realization-dependent random variables. The figure as drawn cannot support the ranking it is used to justify.
  - [BLOCKING] The gray "true spectrum (shape only)" region in (e–h) is overlaid on a kernel axis labeled in "dB re peak" with no vertical calibration. Two quantities with incommensurate normalizations share one dB axis, so the visual impression that the kernel skirt sits "below" or "above" the spectrum at a given offset is not meaningful. This is the kind of dual-quantity/one-axis overlay that readers reliably over-interpret.
  - [BLOCKING] The $-80$ dB floor in (e–h) clips exactly the dynamic range the caption's leakage claims depend on (panel g especially). A claim of "$<0.001\%$ beyond $2W$" must be supported by visible data or by an explicit statement that the curve continues below the axis.
  - [ADVISORY] No confidence interval is shown on any estimate, despite $\nu$ being known — a $\chi^2_\nu$ interval is directly computable and would make the bias-vs-variance argument visible rather than asserted.
  - [ADVISORY] Cross-panel error comparison confounds bias with variance because $\nu$ ranges 8.9–17.6.
  - [ADVISORY] Paired structure (one shared record) is not visually conveyed.
  - [ADVISORY] Unequal plotted frequency support across (a–d); blue and orange traces appear to terminate before $f = 0.5$.
  - [ADVISORY] $N$, $W$, and "one realization" live only in the caption; panels are not self-describing if reproduced in a talk or review excerpt.
  - [ADVISORY] Dense oscillatory traces in (b) and (f) risk rendering-dependent apparent floors.
  - [ADVISORY] Legends appear only in (a) and (e); color-to-method mapping in the lower row relies on the reader carrying color memory down the column. The four colors (green/blue/orange/pink) are reasonably CVD-distinguishable, but this should be confirmed.

SUGGESTED FIXES:
  - Replace or supplement each single-realization trace with an **ensemble summary**: plot the pointwise median over $\geq$ 500 Monte Carlo realizations as a solid line, with a shaded 5th–95th percentile band, and retain one thin single-realization trace for texture. Report the four median-absolute-error values as medians with interquartile ranges across the ensemble, and state the ensemble size in the caption.
  - Add an explicit statement of $n$: e.g., "$N = 1024$ samples; 1000 realizations" in the caption, and annotate $N$, $W$, and $\nu$ inside each panel.
  - Add a $\chi^2_\nu$ 95% confidence interval to at least one representative panel (a vertical error bar at a fiducial frequency, or a band around the true PSD) so the reader can judge which departures exceed sampling noise.
  - Equalize variance before ranking, or state the confound: either tune each method to a common $\nu$ (not just a common half-power width) and re-report the errors, or decompose the reported error into bias and variance terms so the Hann-vs-Welch comparison is interpretable.
  - Recalibrate or de-emphasize the gray overlay: give it its own right-hand axis with an explicit scale, or convert it to a light outline annotated "shape only, arbitrary vertical offset," or remove the dB gridlines behind it so it cannot be read against the kernel scale.
  - Extend the lower-row y-limit to $-100$ or $-120$ dB (or add a broken-axis inset) so the Hann and Welch kernel tails are visible rather than clipped; if a curve still exits the axis, mark the exit point with an arrow and label.
  - Extend the lower-row x-range, or add a small inset showing the cumulative fraction of kernel mass versus offset out to the Nyquist limit. That inset would directly display the "% beyond $2W$" statistic instead of asking the reader to infer it from a truncated window.
  - Make the paired design explicit: add a fifth panel overlaying all four estimates from the same realization on one axis against the true PSD, or plot the four residuals (estimate minus true, in dB) on a shared axis so the common realization noise is visible.
  - Plot all four estimates over the identical frequency support, or state in the caption why the multitaper and Hann traces terminate early.
  - For the dense traces, decimate for display with an explicit min/max envelope per pixel column (or use partial transparency) so the drawn floor level is faithful at any output resolution, and state the decimation in the caption.

---

### figure7.png

---

**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Overlaid line plots are the correct idiom for power spectral density estimates: the x-axis (frequency) is continuous and densely sampled, so no distributional plot (box/violin/beeswarm) is warranted. Panel (b) plotting differences rather than requiring the reader to subtract by eye is good practice. The checklist items on bar graphs, pie charts, and error-bar-hidden distributions do not apply here.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Partially visible — The effective sample size that matters for a spectral estimate, the equivalent degrees of freedom (ν = 14.0, 8.9, 17.6), is printed in the legend of panel (a) and is the single most useful piece of "n" information for this figure. What is *not* visible from the figure is that this is a **single** EEG epoch (n = 1 record); a reader seeing only the panels could assume an ensemble average. Also not inferable: record length, sampling rate, number of raw Fourier bins (Rayleigh resolution), and the half-power widths quoted in the caption.

**COMPARISON:** Valid — This is inherently paired data (three estimators evaluated on the identical epoch at identical frequencies), and panel (b) is the correct paired visualization: within-frequency differences with a zero reference line drawn. This is exactly what item 3 asks for and is done well. One concern: the comparison is anchored to the multitaper estimate as if it were ground truth, but the caption itself notes the seventh Slepian taper's side lobes contaminate the valleys — i.e. the reference is itself biased in precisely the regions where the largest differences occur. The panel-(b) framing therefore risks reading as "error of the other two methods" rather than "mutual disagreement."

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present (clipping, not baseline truncation) — The y-axes are in dB, a log scale, so the absence of a zero baseline is not misleading and is correct practice. However, data are cut off by the axis limits in both panels:
- Panel (b): the orange (smooth − multitaper) trace near 0.7–0.9 Hz runs off the bottom of the axes; its true excursion is unknown to the reader and is visually understated. Since this is the single largest disagreement in the figure, clipping it materially softens the "they agree" message.
- Panel (a): the peaks near 8–11 Hz sit very close to the top frame line with no tick above 40, so it is not possible to confirm nothing is clipped there either.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (line occlusion) — With three traces at equal linewidth, the blue multitaper curve is substantially hidden beneath the pink Welch curve across much of 2–25 Hz (they are the two closest estimates). A reader cannot judge the blue–pink agreement, which is one of the figure's claims. Standard curve-occlusion remedies apply: plot the reference trace last or thicker-and-lighter underneath, add mild transparency, or use distinguishable dash patterns. Note also that the blue/orange/pink set is only marginally safe for deuteranopic readers where blue and pink overlap.

**MULTIPLE COMPARISONS:** No concern — No pairwise significance annotations, brackets, asterisks, or p-values appear anywhere in the figure. The summary statistics (median |diff|, 95th percentile) are descriptive. Worth noting for the authors' own reasoning rather than the figure: adjacent frequency points are strongly correlated by the smoothing bandwidth, so the ~hundreds of plotted differences represent only a few dozen independent comparisons — relevant if any percentile-based claim is later framed inferentially.

**TIME SERIES:** Not applicable — The abscissa is frequency, uniformly sampled from 0 to 40 Hz with no gaps, so there is no misleading interpolation across missing timepoints.

**ISSUES:**
- [BLOCKING] Panel (b): the orange trace is clipped by the lower y-limit near 0.7–0.9 Hz. Data extending beyond the plotted range, at the location of the figure's largest disagreement, is a presentation choice that flatters the stated conclusion. The extremum must be either shown or explicitly annotated.
- [BLOCKING] The headline claim "agree to within their own noise" cannot be verified from the figure. The noise scale (4.3√(2/ν) = 1.6, 2.1, 1.5 dB) exists only as text in the caption; panel (b) carries no reference band against which the plotted differences can be compared. The reader is asked to take the central result on trust.
- [ADVISORY] n = 1 epoch is not evident from the figure; a reader may mistake these for ensemble-averaged spectra.
- [ADVISORY] Blue trace is largely occluded by pink over the 2–25 Hz range, obscuring the multitaper/Welch agreement.
- [ADVISORY] Possible clipping of panel-(a) peaks at the upper frame; no tick above 40 to confirm.
- [ADVISORY] Spectral resolution is not indicated graphically, so the reader cannot tell how many independent points the curves contain or whether the valley structure is resolved.
- [ADVISORY] Caption calls the third trace "purple"; it renders as pink/magenta. The panel-(b) y-axis label appears to collide with the "(b)" panel letter.
- [ADVISORY] The multitaper estimate is used as the comparison baseline while the caption simultaneously identifies it as side-lobe-contaminated in the valleys, an internal tension in the framing.

**SUGGESTED FIXES:**
- Expand the panel-(b) y-limits to contain the full orange excursion; if compressing the informative range is unacceptable, use a broken axis or an inset, or mark the clipped point with a downward arrow annotated with its true value (e.g. "−22 dB at 0.8 Hz").
- Add horizontal shaded bands at ±1 and ±2 × 4.3√(2/ν) to panel (b), one per comparison, so "within their own noise" becomes directly readable. This single change converts the figure's main claim from assertion to demonstration.
- State n in the figure: annotate panel (a) with epoch length, sampling rate, and "single epoch" (e.g. "one 13.5 s epoch, fs = 250 Hz").
- Draw a horizontal bar of length equal to the half-power width (0.91/1.03/0.97 Hz) in a corner of panel (a), plus a tick or marker showing raw bin spacing, so resolution and effective point count are visible.
- Resolve occlusion: draw multitaper as a thick pale line beneath, with smooth and Welch as thinner saturated lines on top; or add dash patterns and check the palette under a deuteranopia simulation.
- Raise the panel-(a) upper y-limit and add a tick at 50 to demonstrate no peaks are clipped.
- Consider a small third panel, or a supplementary figure, showing the distribution of median |diff| across many epochs as a dot/beeswarm plot with individual epochs visible. This is where a distributional plot genuinely belongs and would establish that the single-epoch agreement is typical rather than anecdotal.
- Either relabel panel (b) neutrally (e.g. "pairwise difference, dB") or add a brief note that the multitaper trace is a reference of convenience rather than ground truth; shade the valley regions where its side-lobe bias is expected.
- Fix the caption colour name ("purple" → "pink") and the y-label/panel-letter collision.

### figure8.png

---

**CONTEXT NOTE:** This is a signal-processing / methods-demonstration figure (single EEG record, three spectral estimators). Criteria 1–3 and 6 of the standard checklist largely do not apply in their usual group-comparison sense, but several analogous integrity issues are present and are reported below.

**PLOT TYPE:** Appropriate — Time series (a,b), spectrograms (c,e,g), overlaid spectra (d,f) and a histogram (h) are all the right vehicles for these data; no bar-with-error-bar or pie-chart problems. Two design concerns: (i) the three-estimator comparison in (c,e,g) requires the reader to compare three colour fields by eye, which is perceptually unreliable — a signed difference spectrogram (e minus g, diverging colormap centred at 0) would make the claim directly visible; (ii) panel (h) collapses all time–frequency structure into one marginal distribution, so where the estimators disagree (ictal vs. interictal, low vs. high frequency) is invisible.

**SAMPLE SIZE:** Not visible — The figure itself shows no n. The caption states 599 windows, but nothing in the figure conveys: the number of subjects/seizures (apparently n = 1 record, 1 channel, 1 seizure), the number of pixels entering the (h) histogram, or the frequency-bin count. Effective n in (h) is also much smaller than the pixel count because 2-s windows stepped by 1 s overlap 50 % and neighbouring frequency bins are correlated by the smoothing/taper bandwidth — the histogram therefore represents far fewer independent observations than its counts imply.

**COMPARISON:** Concern — (c,e,g), (d,f) and (h) are intrinsically *paired* comparisons (identical data, different estimators), but only (h) exploits the pairing, and only in aggregate. Panels (d,f) are correctly overlaid (a good paired presentation). Panels (c,e,g) are presented side-by-side (unpaired-style) when a paired difference map is available and more informative. Additionally, (d) and (f) use different y-ranges (≈ −10–45 dB vs ≈ 25–85 dB), so the apparent smoothing benefit cannot be compared across the two instants.

**AXIS TRUNCATION:** Present — Two distinct forms:
- Panel (a) is explicitly **clipped at ±500 µV**, which flattens the ictal burst; panel (b), drawn from inside that burst, plainly exceeds −500 µV. A reader comparing (a) and (b) is given inconsistent amplitude information. Clipping is disclosed in the caption but not on the figure.
- The shared colour scale in (c,e,g) **saturates at 45 dB**, so the entire ictal block renders as uniform yellow in all three panels. This is colour-axis truncation: it removes exactly the region where the estimators are claimed to differ most, and it makes the three spectrograms look more similar than they are.
- No misleading truncation of the frequency axes; (h) appears to be cut at roughly ±8 dB — confirm that no tail mass is being clipped off-axis.

**OVERPLOTTING:** Present (mild) — In (d) and (f) the thick blue multitaper trace overlays and partly hides the orange Hann-then-box and pink Welch traces, and the grey periodogram is buried beneath all of them in the low-frequency region. Line-order/z-order determines what the reader sees.
